\medterm{Post-Mortem} An examination of a body after death to determine the cause of death, identify disease
processes, and evaluate the effects of medical or surgical treatment. Also called
autopsy, necropsy.

\textbf{Synonyms}: autopsy, necropsy.

\medterm{Post-Obstructive Pneumonia} Pneumonia occurring distal to a bronchial obstruction caused by tumor, foreign body,
mucus plug, or stenosis. The obstruction prevents clearance of secretions, creating a
favorable environment for infection. It does not resolve with antibiotics alone until
the obstruction is relieved.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Post-Operative Atelectasis} Collapse of some alveoli after surgery, promoted by anesthesia, shallow breathing, pain,
immobility, mucus retention, or underlying lung disease. Management addresses the cause and
may include mobilization, effective analgesia, deep breathing, and treatment of complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Post-Pneumonectomy Syndrome} A rare complication following pneumonectomy where the remaining lung herniates across
the mediastinum, causing compression of airways and vessels. It presents with dyspnea,
stridor, and recurrent infections. Treatment is surgical correction with mediastinal
repositioning and prosthetic implant placement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Post-Streptococcal Glomerulonephritis} Immune-mediated glomerulonephritis following infection with nephritogenic strains of
group A streptococcus typically 1 to 3 weeks after pharyngitis or 3 to 6 weeks after
skin infection. Subepithelial humps on electron microscopy representing immune complex
deposits.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Post-Surgical Pain} Acute pain following operative procedures, caused by tissue incision, retraction,
dissection, and inflammation, mediated by nociceptors sensitized by prostaglandins,
bradykinin, and cytokines. Intensity varies by procedure (thoracotomy, joint
replacement, and laparotomy produce more severe pain).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Post-Surgical Rehabilitation} Recovery program after surgery. Phases: acute (pain control, wound care), subacute
(range of motion, strengthening), functional (return to activities). Protocols vary by
procedure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Post-Term Pregnancy} Pregnancy continuing beyond 42 weeks gestation (294 days from LMP). Risk factors include
prior post-term pregnancy, nulliparity, and genetic factors. Complications include
macrosomia, oligohydramnios, meconium aspiration, placental insufficiency, and
stillbirth.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Post-Test Probability} The probability of disease after incorporating a test result with the pretest
probability. It can be estimated using Bayes’ theorem or by converting pretest odds to
post-test odds with the appropriate likelihood ratio.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Post-Thrombotic Syndrome} Chronic pain, heaviness, edema, skin change, and sometimes ulceration developing after
deep-vein thrombosis because of persistent venous obstruction and valvular damage.
Compression, exercise, skin care, and selected interventions may reduce disability.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Post-Transfusion Purpura} A rare immune-mediated thrombocytopenic disorder occurring several days after
transfusion of blood products, usually in previously sensitized individuals. It causes
abrupt, severe thrombocytopenia with petechiae, mucosal bleeding, or more serious
haemorrhage.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Post-Traumatic Stress Disorder} A disorder that may develop after exposure to actual or threatened death, serious injury,
or sexual violence, causing persistent re-experiencing, avoidance, negative mood or
cognition, and heightened arousal.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Post-Void Residual} The amount of urine remaining in the bladder after urination, measured by ultrasound or
catheterization. A persistently high residual may indicate incomplete emptying, but its
significance depends on the volume, timing, symptoms, and clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Post-Void Residual Volume} The amount of urine remaining in the bladder immediately after urination, measured by
ultrasound or catheterization. An elevated residual suggests incomplete emptying but
must be interpreted with timing, bladder volume, symptoms, and repeated measurements.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postanesthesia Care Unit} The postanesthesia care unit (PACU) is a monitored recovery area where patients are
observed during the immediate postoperative period. Admission assessment includes vital
signs, level of consciousness, pain assessment, and surgical site evaluation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postcholecystectomy Syndrome} Persistence or recurrence of symptoms after cholecystectomy. Causes include retained
common bile duct stones, sphincter of Oddi dysfunction, bile salt malabsorption,
irritable bowel syndrome, and other gastrointestinal conditions. Evaluation includes
liver function tests, MRCP, and endoscopic ultrasound.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Posterior} Relating to the back of the body or positioned closer to the back.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Posterior Cruciate Ligament Injury} Tear of the PCL, less common than ACL injury. Mechanism: dashboard injury (tibia
striking dashboard), hyperflexion, or fall on a flexed knee with foot plantarflexed.
Presents with posterior knee pain, mild swelling, and posterior sag of the tibia.
Positive posterior drawer test (most sensitive), posterior sag sign (Godfrey test).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Posterior Cruciate Ligament Tear} PCL tear: less common, usually from direct blow to tibia (dashboard injury). Symptoms:
knee pain, swelling, instability. Diagnosis: posterior drawer test, MRI. Treatment:
usually conservative, surgical if combined injuries.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Posterior Descending Artery} An artery running in the posterior interventricular groove and supplying the inferior
ventricular walls and posterior interventricular septum. It usually arises from the
right coronary artery in right-dominant circulation but may arise from the circumflex
artery.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Posterior Drawer Test} PCL integrity test. Knee flexed 90 degrees, posterior tibial translation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Posterior Pituitary} The neural lobe of the pituitary that stores and releases vasopressin and oxytocin
synthesized in the hypothalamus. Vasopressin regulates renal water retention and
vascular tone; oxytocin contributes to uterine contraction and milk ejection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Posterior reversible encephalopathy syndrome} A neurotoxic state caused by failed cerebrovascular autoregulation, typically from acute
hypertension, eclampsia, immunosuppressive drugs (tacrolimus, cyclosporine), or sepsis.
Presents with headache, seizures, altered consciousness, and visual disturbances.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Posterior Subcapsular Cataract} A type of lens opacity located at the posterior aspect of the lens, just anterior to the
posterior lens capsule, in the visual axis, and it is particularly visually disabling
because of its central location.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Posterior Tibial Artery} Arises from the popliteal artery, courses deep in the posterior leg, palpable behind the
medial malleolus. Divides into medial/lateral plantar arteries for the sole. Supplies
posterior leg muscles. Important for peripheral vascular disease assessment. The pedal
pulse behind the medial malleolus is a key clinical examination component.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Posterior Urethral Valves} The most common cause of lower urinary tract obstruction in male infants, caused by
congenital mucosal folds (valves) in the posterior urethra (prostatic urethra) that
obstruct urine outflow.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Posterior Uveitis} Inflammation affecting the retina, choroid, or both. It may cause floaters, blurred vision, visual-field defects, or
eye pain and can result from infection, autoimmune disease, or other inflammatory
conditions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Posterior Vitreous Detachment} Separation of the posterior vitreous cortex from the retinal surface, commonly related
to age-related vitreous liquefaction. New flashes or floaters require dilated retinal
examination because an associated retinal tear or detachment may be present.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postmenopausal osteoporosis} Bone loss caused by lower estrogen levels associated with menopause. Sometimes called
type I osteoporosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postmenopause} The period in a woman’s life lasting from the end of perimenopause until the end of
life.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postmortem Artifact} Any structural, chemical, or physical change introduced into a body after death (e.g.,
embalming artifacts, insect activity, postmortem animal scavenging, resuscitation
trauma) that mimics antemortem disease or trauma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postmortem Interval} Estimated elapsed time between actual death and the time of autopsy or body discovery,
calculated utilizing algor mortis, livor mortis, rigor mortis, vitreous potassium
levels, and insect succession patterns.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postmortem Toxicology} Toxicologic analysis of specimens collected after death to identify drugs, alcohols,
poisons, and metabolites. Results must account for therapeutic administration,
redistribution, decomposition, specimen site, contamination, and tolerance before
conclusions about causation are made.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postnatal depression} A depressive episode occurring after childbirth, with persistent low mood, anhedonia,
sleep or appetite disturbance, guilt, impaired bonding, or suicidal thoughts.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postoperative Care} Structured post-surgical management encompassing pain control, fluid balance, wound
care, early mobilization, and venous thromboembolism prophylaxis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postoperative Cognitive Dysfunction} A decline in cognitive abilities such as memory, attention, or executive function after
surgery. It may be temporary or persistent and is more frequent in older adults and after
major or complicated procedures. Assessment requires comparison with the person's baseline
and consideration of delirium, medication effects, and other medical causes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postoperative Complications} Clavien-Dindo classification: Grade I (any deviation, no treatment), Grade II
(pharmacologic), Grade III (surgical/endoscopic/radiologic intervention: IIIa no GA,
IIIb GA), Grade IV (ICU: IVa single organ, IVb multi-organ), Grade V (death).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postoperative Delirium} A common complication after major surgery, particularly in elderly patients. It is
categorized as hyperactive (agitation, restlessness, hallucinations), hypoactive
(lethargy, withdrawal, decreased responsiveness), or mixed. Hypoactive delirium is the
most common and most missed form.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postoperative Ileus} Temporary impairment of gastrointestinal motility after surgery, most common after
abdominal surgery. Pathophysiology: autonomic dysfunction (sympathetic overactivity),
inflammatory mediators, opioids, and electrolyte abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postoperative Nausea and Vomiting} Nausea, retching, or vomiting occurring after surgery. Risk is influenced by factors
such as previous motion sickness or postoperative nausea, anesthetic technique, opioid
use, and patient characteristics. Prevention and treatment are tailored to the individual
risk and procedure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postoperative Pain Management} Management of pain after surgery using a combination of medicines, regional
techniques, physical measures, and rehabilitation. The plan is tailored to the procedure, pain severity, medical
conditions, and risks of adverse effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postoperative Pulmonary Complication} Respiratory deterioration after surgery, including atelectasis, pneumonia, aspiration,
respiratory failure, bronchospasm, and exacerbation of chronic lung disease. Risk
reduction includes smoking cessation, lung-protective ventilation, analgesia,
mobilization, and appropriate respiratory exercises.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postpartum Anxiety} Excessive, persistent, or distressing anxiety during pregnancy or after childbirth that interferes with sleep, daily
function, bonding, or self-care. It may occur with panic, intrusive worries, or depression and deserves clinical
assessment; thoughts of self-harm or harm to the baby require emergency help.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postpartum Blues} A common, short-lived period of tearfulness, mood changes, irritability, or anxiety beginning
soon after childbirth and usually improving within two weeks. Support, rest, and practical
help are important; persistent, severe, psychotic, or suicidal symptoms require prompt
assessment for a postpartum mental-health disorder.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postpartum Cardiomyopathy (Peripartum Cardiomyopathy)} A dilated cardiomyopathy causing heart failure toward
the end of pregnancy or during the months after delivery. Symptoms may include breathlessness, difficulty lying flat,
swelling, fatigue, or chest discomfort and require prompt assessment.



\textbf{Synonyms}: Peripartum Cardiomyopathy

\medterm{Postpartum Depression} Major depressive or manic episodes occurring during pregnancy or within four weeks of
delivery (DSM-5). The term is often used more broadly to cover depressive episodes up to
one year postpartum. Risk factors include prior depression, lack of social support,
stressful life events, and hormonal fluctuations.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postpartum Endometritis} Infection of the decidua and endometrial cavity after delivery, occurring more often
after cesarean birth. Fever, uterine tenderness, pelvic pain, and foul-smelling lochia
are typical; treatment generally requires intravenous broad-spectrum antibiotics.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postpartum Hemorrhage} Excessive bleeding after childbirth, usually caused by poor uterine contraction, retained placental tissue, genital-tract
trauma, or a clotting disorder. It can cause shock and requires urgent obstetric
assessment and treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postpartum Hemorrhage (Secondary)} Excessive bleeding occurring between 24 hours and 12 weeks postpartum. Causes: retained
products of conception (most common), endometritis, subinvolution of the placental site,
and coagulopathy. Presents with heavy or prolonged bleeding, cramping, and passing
clots. Diagnosis: ultrasound (retained tissue, thickened endometrium).



\textbf{Synonyms}: Secondary

\medterm{Postpartum Infection} Infections occurring after delivery, a leading cause of maternal morbidity. Types:
endometritis (most common, especially after cesarean), wound infection (cesarean
incision, episiotomy, perineal laceration), urinary tract infection, mastitis, septic
pelvic thrombophlebitis, and pneumonia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postpartum Psychosis} A rare but severe psychiatric emergency (1-2 per 1000 deliveries) characterized by the
acute onset of delusions, hallucinations, disorganized behavior, confusion, and mood
swings (mania or depression) within 1-4 weeks postpartum.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postpartum Thyroiditis} Autoimmune inflammation of the thyroid occurring during the first year after delivery. It may cause a
temporary hyperthyroid phase followed by hypothyroidism, and symptoms and recovery vary;
thyroid-function testing guides follow-up and treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postrenal AKI} Acute kidney injury caused by obstruction of urine flow from the kidneys to the urethra. Causes include prostate
enlargement, stones, tumors, fibrosis, and neurogenic bladder. The effect depends on the
location, duration, completeness, and whether one or both kidneys are affected.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Poststreptococcal pustulosis} A sterile, non-follicular pustular eruption occurring as a rare sequela of streptococcal
pharyngitis, presenting with scattered pustules on an erythematous base, typically on
the trunk and extremities. It is thought to be an immune-mediated hypersensitivity
reaction to streptococcal antigens.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postterm Pregnancy} Pregnancy that continues beyond 42 completed weeks of gestation. Risks and management
depend on gestational dating, fetal condition, and maternal health.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Posttraumatic Stress Disorder} A psychiatric disorder that develops after exposure to a traumatic event (death, serious
injury, sexual violence).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postural Orthostatic Tachycardia Syndrome} An autonomic disorder causing an excessive increase in heart rate after standing, with symptoms
such as light-headedness, palpitations, fatigue, weakness, brain fog, nausea, or fainting. Diagnosis requires
clinical assessment and exclusion of other causes of orthostatic symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Potassium} Major intracellular cation. Essential for cardiac function, muscle contraction, blood
pressure regulation. Deficiency: hypokalemia (weakness, arrhythmias). Sources: fruits,
vegetables, potatoes, beans.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Potassium Binders} Sodium zirconium cyclosilicate and patiromer. Exchange potassium in GI tract for sodium
or calcium. Treat chronic hyperkalemia allowing continued RAAS inhibitor use in CKD.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Potassium-Sparing Diuretics} Potassium-sparing diuretics act on the distal nephron to promote sodium excretion while
minimizing potassium loss. They include epithelial sodium channel blockers such as
amiloride and triamterene, and mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists such as
spironolactone and eplerenone.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Potency} The amount of a drug required to produce a specified effect. A more potent drug produces that effect at a
lower dose, but potency does not indicate the maximum effect.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Potency vs Efficacy} Potency is the amount of drug required to produce a given effect, measured by the EC50
(the concentration producing 50\% of maximal effect). Drugs with higher potency achieve
effects at lower doses (e.g., fentanyl is more potent than morphine).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pott Disease} TB of the spine involving thoracic or lumbar vertebrae. Causes vertebral destruction,
kyphosis (gibbus deformity), and paravertebral abscess. May cause spinal cord
compression.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Potter Syndrome} A sequence of anomalies resulting from severe oligohydramnios (low amniotic fluid
volume), most commonly caused by bilateral renal agenesis, but also from autosomal
recessive polycystic kidney disease, posterior urethral valves, or chronic amniotic
fluid leak.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Powassan Encephalitis} Encephalitis caused by Powassan virus, a tick-borne flavivirus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{PR Interval} The time from the onset of the P wave to the onset of the QRS complex, representing the
time for electrical conduction through the atria, AV node, and His-Purkinje system.
Normal duration is 0.12-0.20 seconds (3-5 small boxes). Prolonged PR interval (>0.20 s)
indicates first-degree AV block.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prader-Willi Syndrome} A genetic disorder caused by loss of function of genes on chromosome 15 (paternal
deletion or maternal uniparental disomy). Features: neonatal hypotonia and failure to
thrive, followed by hyperphagia and obesity in childhood. Intellectual disability, short
stature, hypogonadism, and behavioral problems.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pralidoxime} An oxime that can reactivate inhibited acetylcholinesterase when administered early
after organophosphate poisoning, particularly for nicotinic weakness and
respiratory-muscle dysfunction. It is used with atropine and supportive care according
to exposure and local guidance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pramipexole} A non-ergot dopamine agonist (D2/D3) used for Parkinson disease (as monotherapy in early
disease or adjunct to levodopa) and restless legs syndrome. Activates striatal dopamine
receptors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prebiotics} Non-digestible fibers feeding beneficial gut bacteria. Sources: garlic, onions, leeks,
asparagus, bananas, whole grains.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prebiotics and Probiotics} Prebiotics are nondigestible food ingredients (fiber and oligosaccharides—inulin,
fructooligosaccharides, galactooligosaccharides) that selectively stimulate the growth
and activity of beneficial gut bacteria (Bifidobacterium, Lactobacillus), promoting host
health.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Precision Medicine} Treatment tailored to individual tumor characteristics. Requires molecular profiling.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Precocious Puberty} Onset of secondary sexual characteristics before age eight in girls and age nine in
boys. Central precocious puberty results from early activation of the
hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis. Causes include CNS tumors, hydrocephalus, and
idiopathic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Preconception Counseling} Counseling provided to women of reproductive age before pregnancy to optimize outcomes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prediabetes} Intermediate state of hyperglycemia between normal glucose homeostasis and diabetes
defined by fasting glucose 100 to 125 milligrams per deciliter, two-hour glucose during
oral glucose tolerance test 140 to 199 milligrams per deciliter, or A1c 5.7 to 6.4
percent.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Predictive Test} A test that estimates whether a person or disease is likely to respond to a particular treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Preeclampsia} A pregnancy-specific hypertensive disorder defined as new-onset hypertension (blood
pressure 140/90 mmHg or higher) after 20 weeks gestation with proteinuria or other
end-organ damage.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Preeclampsia with Severe Features} Preeclampsia accompanied by severe hypertension or signs of significant organ involvement, such as low platelets,
liver or kidney dysfunction, pulmonary edema, severe headache, or visual symptoms. It
requires urgent obstetric evaluation and management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pregnancy} The physiological state in which a developing embryo or fetus grows within the female
uterus, typically lasting approximately 40 weeks from the last menstrual period to
childbirth.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pregnancy Nutrition} Nutrition care during pregnancy that supports maternal health, placental and fetal
growth, and appropriate gestational weight gain. Energy, protein, folate, iron, iodine,
choline, vitamin D, and other requirements vary by stage and individual clinical
factors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pregnancy Test} A glycoprotein hormone produced by the placenta, detected in urine and serum for
pregnancy diagnosis. See hCG (Human Chorionic Gonadotropin).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pregnancy-Induced Hypertension} New-onset hypertension during pregnancy without the proteinuria or organ
dysfunction that defines preeclampsia. Diagnosis and treatment depend on repeated blood-pressure measurements,
gestational age, symptoms, and evidence of maternal or fetal complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Preload} The end-diastolic volume or pressure in the ventricles, representing the degree of
ventricular stretch before contraction. Preload is determined by venous return and is
estimated by the pulmonary capillary wedge pressure (PCWP) or left ventricular
end-diastolic pressure (LVEDP).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Premature (Early) Ejaculation} A persistent or recurrent pattern of ejaculation that occurs sooner than desired,
with limited control and associated personal distress. Assessment considers the person's typical experience and sexual
context.



\textbf{Synonyms}: Early

\medterm{Premature Atrial Contraction} A premature beat arising from an ectopic focus in the atria, producing an early P wave
(often abnormal morphology) followed by a normal QRS; common and usually benign,
increasing with age and caffeine/stress. ECG: early abnormal P wave, normal PR and QRS,
followed by a compensatory pause.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Premature Ejaculation} Ejaculation occurring sooner than desired. Most common sexual dysfunction in men.
Treatment: behavioral techniques, topical anesthetics, SSRIs, counseling.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Premature Ovarian Insufficiency} Loss of ovarian function before age forty characterized by amenorrhea, hypergonadotropic
hypogonadism, and estrogen deficiency. Causes include genetic disorders particularly
Turner syndrome and fragile X premutations, autoimmune oophoritis, iatrogenic from
chemotherapy or radiation, and idiopathic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Premature Rupture of Membranes} PROM: spontaneous rupture of the amniotic membranes before the onset of labor; preterm
PROM (PPROM) occurs before 37 weeks. Features: gush or leakage of fluid per vaginam;
confirmed by sterile speculum (pooling, ferning, nitrazine pH test) or amniotic fluid
biomarkers (IGFBP-1, PAMG-1).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Premature Ventricular Contractions} Ectopic beats originating from the ventricles, appearing as wide, bizarre QRS complexes
that occur earlier than expected. PVCs are common in the general population and are
usually benign. Frequent PVCs (>10\% of total beats) may cause left ventricular
dysfunction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Premenstrual Dysphoric Disorder} A severe, debilitating form of premenstrual syndrome characterized by marked mood
symptoms (depression, anxiety, irritability, mood swings), physical symptoms (breast
tenderness, bloating, fatigue), and functional impairment that occur during the luteal
phase and remit within days of menses onset.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Premenstrual Syndrome} A recurrent pattern of physical, emotional, and behavioral symptoms during the luteal
phase of the menstrual cycle that resolve with menses. Affects up to 50\% of
reproductive-age women. Symptoms: irritability, mood swings, depression, anxiety,
bloating, breast tenderness, fatigue, and appetite changes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prenatal Care} Regular medical care during pregnancy to monitor maternal and fetal health. Includes
history and physical exam, weight and blood pressure monitoring, urine testing, blood
work, fundal height measurements, fetal heart rate auscultation, and ultrasound.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prenatal Diagnosis} Detection of genetic or structural disorders in fetus before birth. Methods: chorionic
villus sampling (CVS, 10-13 weeks), amniocentesis (15-20 weeks), percutaneous umbilical
blood sampling (PUBS, after 18 weeks). Non-invasive: NIPT (cell-free fetal DNA).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prenatal Exposure} Contact of a fetus with a medicine, substance, infection, or environmental factor during pregnancy.
Effects depend on the agent, dose, timing, and fetal susceptibility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prenatal Vitamins} Multivitamin supplements formulated for pregnancy, containing folic acid (400-800 mcg),
iron (27-60 mg), calcium (200-300 mg), vitamin D (400-600 IU), DHA (200 mg), and iodine
(150-220 mcg). Started preconceptionally or at the first prenatal visit. Evidence
supports NTD reduction from folic acid. Iron prevents maternal anemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Preoperative Assessment} Preoperative assessment evaluates surgical risk and optimizes medical conditions before
anesthesia. It includes medical history, physical examination, airway assessment,
cardiovascular risk stratification ( Revised Cardiac Risk Index ), pulmonary function
assessment, and laboratory testing based on clinical indication.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Preoperative Evaluation} Medical assessment before elective surgery to optimize outcomes and minimize
perioperative risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prerenal Azotemia} A rise in nitrogen-containing waste products caused by reduced effective blood flow to the
kidneys without primary renal-tissue injury. Causes include dehydration, hemorrhage, heart failure, sepsis, and
hepatorenal syndrome; urine indices may support but do not by themselves establish the diagnosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Presbycusis} Bilateral, usually symmetric age-related sensorineural hearing loss, initially affecting
high frequencies and speech discrimination in noise. Management includes hearing
rehabilitation, hearing aids, communication strategies, and cochlear implantation in
selected severe cases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Presbyopia} Age-related loss of accommodation caused by lens hardening and loss of elasticity,
beginning around age 40 and causing progressive difficulty with near vision. Corrected
with reading glasses, bifocals, or progressive lenses; the most common refractive change
of aging.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prescription} A written or electronic order by a licensed prescriber (physician, NP, PA, dentist)
authorizing a pharmacy to dispense a specific drug to a specific patient. Components:
patient identifiers, drug name, dose, route, frequency, quantity, refills, and
prescriber signature.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prescription Drug} A medication whose dispensing or use is subject to prescription requirements under the applicable law. It
requires professional oversight because of its risks, complexity, or need for monitoring;
regulatory approval and prescribing rules vary by country and product.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prescription Medications} Pharmaceutical drugs that require authorization from a licensed healthcare provider
before they can be dispensed to a patient. These medications are regulated because they
carry risks that necessitate professional oversight, including potential side effects,
drug interactions, and the need for precise dosing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Preseptal Cellulitis} Preseptal cellulitis, also known as periorbital cellulitis, is an infection of the
eyelid and periorbital tissues anterior to the orbital septum, without involvement of
the orbital contents, and it is the most common orbital infection, particularly in
children.

\textbf{Synonyms}: periorbital cellulitis, is an infection of the.

\medterm{Pressure Atrophy} The gradual wasting of tissue resulting from sustained external compression that impairs
blood supply and causes cellular shrinkage and eventual death. The mechanism involves
compression of blood vessels, which reduces perfusion and causes ischemia of the
dependent tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pressure Injury} Localized damage to the skin and/or underlying soft tissue caused by prolonged pressure,
shear, or friction, most commonly over bony prominences.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pressure Support Ventilation} Spontaneous breathing mode with ventilator-provided pressure support above PEEP for each
breath. Reduces work of breathing. Used for weaning from mechanical ventilation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pressure Ulcer} Localized damage to skin and underlying tissue caused by sustained pressure or pressure
with shear, typically over a bony prominence. Staging includes non-blanchable erythema,
partial-thickness loss, full-thickness loss, and full-thickness tissue loss with exposed
or palpable deep structures; obscured wounds are unstageable.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pressure Ulcer Prevention} Measures that reduce prolonged pressure and tissue injury, including repositioning, support
surfaces, skin inspection, moisture control, nutrition, and mobility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pressure Urticaria} A form of chronic inducible urticaria in which sustained pressure causes localized
swelling, erythema, and pain, usually after a delay of several hours. Lesions may last 8
to 72 hours and commonly affect the hands, feet, buttocks, or areas compressed by
clothing or sitting.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pressure-Flow Study} Suite of diagnostic procedures designed to evaluate the physiological function of the
lower urinary tract (bladder and urethral sphincter) during filling, storage, and
emptying phases. See urodynamic testing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Presyncope} The sensation of imminent fainting (lightheadedness, weakness, visual blurring) without
actual loss of consciousness, due to transient global cerebral hypoperfusion that
resolves before LOC. Causes: orthostatic hypotension (volume depletion, autonomic
failure, medications), vasovagal, cardiac (arrhythmias, aortic stenosis), and metabolic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Preterm Birth} Birth before 37 completed weeks of gestation. It may follow spontaneous labor or rupture of the
membranes, or may be medically indicated because of maternal or fetal complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Preterm Infant} An infant born before 37 completed weeks of gestation; classified by gestational age
(late preterm 34-36, moderate 32-33, very preterm <32, extremely <28 weeks) and by birth
weight. Causes: preterm labor, PPROM, preeclampsia, multiple pregnancy, infection, and
iatrogenic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Preterm Labor} Regular uterine contractions causing cervical change before 37 weeks gestation. Risk
factors include prior preterm delivery, multiple gestation, infection, and cervical
insufficiency. Diagnosis requires regular contractions with documented cervical change.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Preterm Premature Rupture of Membranes} Rupture of the amniotic membranes before the onset of labor at <37 weeks gestation.
Causes: infection (subclinical chorioamnionitis), cervical insufficiency, and prior
PPROM. Presents with sudden gush or leakage of fluid per vagina.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pretest Probability} The estimated probability that a patient has a disease before the result of a diagnostic
test is known. It is based on prevalence, symptoms, examination findings, risk factors,
and prior test results and should guide test selection and interpretation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pretibial Myxedema} Localized dermopathy occurring in approximately five percent of patients with Graves
disease caused by glycosaminoglycan deposition in the dermis particularly over the
pretibial area. Presents as non-pitting indurated plaques with a peau dorange
appearance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prevalence} Total number of cases (new and existing) in population at given time. Formula: all cases
/ total population. Prevalence = incidence × duration.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prevention} Measures to prevent disease. Primary (before disease), secondary (early detection),
tertiary (complication prevention).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Preventive Dentistry} Practice of preventing dental disease. Methods: oral hygiene instruction, fluoride,
sealants, diet counseling, regular check-ups.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prevertebral air} Gas in the prevertebral soft tissues on lateral cervical spine radiograph, indicating a
breach in the aerodigestive tract. It may result from esophageal perforation, tracheal
injury, retropharyngeal abscess with gas-forming organisms, or trauma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Priapism} Prolonged, painful erection lasting longer than 4 hours, occurring independently of
sexual desire or stimulation. Medical emergency categorized into two types: ischemic
(low-flow, veno-occlusive) and non-ischemic (high-flow, arterial).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison's disease)} Destruction or dysfunction of the adrenal cortex causing deficiency of cortisol and
aldosterone, presenting with fatigue, hyperpigmentation, weight loss, and salt craving.
Lifelong hormone replacement is required.



\textbf{Synonyms}: Addison's disease

\medterm{Primary adrenal lymphoma} A rare lymphoma arising primarily in the adrenal gland, most commonly diffuse large
B-cell lymphoma. It often presents bilaterally and may cause adrenal insufficiency.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Aldosteronism} Excess production of aldosterone by the adrenal glands, usually from an adrenal nodule or enlargement of both glands.
It increases sodium retention and may cause hypertension, low potassium, or suppressed
renin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Biliary Cholangitis} A chronic autoimmune liver disease in which small intrahepatic bile ducts are
progressively damaged. It can cause cholestasis, itching, fatigue, and eventually cirrhosis; antimitochondrial antibodies
often support the diagnosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Ciliary Dyskinesia} An inherited disorder of ciliary structure or function that impairs mucociliary
clearance. It can cause chronic sinus and lung infections, bronchiectasis, infertility, and abnormal organ laterality.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Hyperaldosteronism} Excess autonomous production of aldosterone by the adrenal glands. It can cause
hypertension, low potassium, metabolic alkalosis, and suppressed renin. Causes include an adrenal adenoma or bilateral
adrenal hyperplasia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary hyperoxaluria} A group of autosomal recessive metabolic disorders characterized by overproduction of
oxalate by the liver, leading to calcium oxalate deposition in the kidneys and other
organs. Type 1 is caused by alanine-glyoxylate aminotransferase deficiency. It presents
with recurrent nephrolithiasis, nephrocalcinosis, and progressive renal failure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Hyperparathyroidism} Autonomous overproduction of parathyroid hormone from one or more abnormal parathyroid
glands causing hypercalcemia. Most commonly caused by a single parathyroid adenoma
accounting for approximately eighty-five percent. Multigland hyperplasia accounts for
approximately ten to fifteen percent and parathyroid carcinoma is rare.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary hypertrophic osteoarthropathy} A rare inherited syndrome characterized by digital clubbing, periostosis of long bones,
and pachydermia, presenting with joint pain, skin thickening, and visible nail-bed
enlargement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Immune Response} The adaptive immune response to first exposure to an antigen, characterized by a slow
onset (lag phase: days, during which antigen processing, clonal expansion, and
differentiation occur), followed by rising antibody titers (predominantly IgM, then
IgG), and generation of memory cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Immunodeficiency} A group of genetically determined disorders of the immune system causing increased
susceptibility to infection, autoimmunity, and malignancy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Lateral Sclerosis} A rare neurodegenerative disorder confined to the upper motor neurons (corticospinal
tract) of the motor cortex and brainstem. Presents with slowly progressive spasticity,
hyperreflexia, Babinski sign, and pseudobulbar affect (emotional lability).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Metabolism} The essential biochemical processes by which cells produce energy, synthesize molecules, and maintain
their functions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Myelofibrosis} A chronic myeloproliferative neoplasm characterized by bone marrow fibrosis (reticulin
and/or collagen fibrosis), extramedullary hematopoiesis (primarily splenic and hepatic),
and a leukoerythroblastic peripheral blood picture.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Open-Angle Glaucoma} A chronic optic neuropathy with characteristic retinal ganglion-cell and optic-nerve
loss, usually accompanied by progressive visual-field defects, despite an anatomically
open anterior-chamber angle. Intraocular pressure is an important modifiable risk
factor, and treatment lowers pressure to slow progression.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Ovarian Insufficiency} Loss of normal ovarian activity before age 40, characterized by irregular or absent
menses, reduced estrogen, and intermittently or persistently elevated gonadotropins.
Causes include genetic, autoimmune, iatrogenic, toxic, infectious, and unknown factors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Prevention} Preventing cancer development. Strategies: smoking cessation, vaccination (HPV,
hepatitis B), sun protection, healthy diet, exercise.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Sclerosing Cholangitis} Chronic cholestatic liver disease characterized by progressive fibrosis and strictures
of intrahepatic and extrahepatic bile ducts. The disease has a strong association with
ulcerative colitis occurring in seventy to eighty percent of patients.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Tuberculosis} The first infection with Mycobacterium tuberculosis. It may produce a small pulmonary focus and
regional lymph-node involvement; the infection may be contained as latent tuberculosis or
progress to active disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Tumor} Original cancer site. Secondary: metastatic sites.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primordial Prevention} Preventing the emergence of disease risk factors by addressing social, economic,
behavioural, and environmental conditions early. Examples: promoting healthy diets and
physical activity in children, reducing tobacco exposure, and creating safe environments
for exercise.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prion} An infectious agent composed solely of misfolded protein (PrPSc) that propagates by
converting normal cellular prion protein (PrPC) into the abnormal conformation; contains
no nucleic acid, resistant to standard sterilization (heat, radiation, formaldehyde).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prion Disease} A neurodegenerative disease caused by abnormal folding and propagation of prion protein. These disorders are
rare and usually progressive and fatal, although their course varies by the specific disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{PRK} Photorefractive keratectomy, commonly known as PRK, is a refractive surgical procedure
that corrects myopia, hyperopia, and astigmatism by reshaping the corneal stroma using
an excimer laser after mechanical removal of the corneal epithelium, without creating a
corneal flap.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Probable Cause} A legally recognized reasonable basis for believing that a person committed an offense or that evidence
of an offense will be found.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Probe} Instrument for exploring wound or cavity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Probiotic} Live microorganisms administered in adequate amounts to confer a health benefit by
modulating the microbiome and immune responses.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Procalcitonin} A biomarker that may rise during some bacterial infections and systemic inflammation. It can support
assessment of infection and antibiotic decisions, but it cannot by itself diagnose or
exclude bacterial infection or determine sepsis severity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Procalcitonin-guided antibiotic therapy} The use of procalcitonin levels to guide initiation and discontinuation of antibiotics.
Procalcitonin is a biomarker that rises in bacterial infections and falls with effective
treatment. Elevated levels support the need for antibiotic therapy, while declining
levels support discontinuation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Procedural Sedation} Administration of sedative and analgesic medicines to facilitate a painful or
anxiety-provoking procedure while preserving spontaneous ventilation and protective
reflexes to the intended depth. Continuous monitoring and immediate rescue capability
are required.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Procedure} A planned medical or surgical action performed for diagnosis, treatment, prevention, or palliation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Proctitis} Inflammation of the rectal mucosa causing tenesmus, rectal pain, and bloody discharge.
Causes include inflammatory bowel disease, sexually transmitted infections including
gonorrhea, chlamydia, and herpes, radiation therapy, and ischemia. Diagnosed by
proctoscopy with biopsy. Treatment is directed at the underlying etiology.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prodrug} A prodrug is a pharmacologically inactive compound that is converted to an active drug
through metabolic processes within the body. Prodrugs are designed to overcome
limitations of the parent drug including poor bioavailability, rapid metabolism, lack of
tissue selectivity, or unacceptable side effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Product Liability} Legal responsibility for injury or damage caused by a defective or unsafe product.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Progeria} A rare genetic premature-aging syndrome, most commonly caused by a pathogenic LMNA
variant. Children develop severe growth failure, characteristic skin and facial changes,
loss of subcutaneous fat, joint stiffness, and early atherosclerotic cardiovascular
disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Progesterone} A natural steroid hormone produced mainly by the corpus luteum after ovulation and by
the placenta during pregnancy. It transforms and maintains the endometrium for
implantation, reduces uterine contractility during pregnancy, supports breast glandular
development, and contributes to regulation of the menstrual cycle.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Progesterone Supplementation} Administration of progesterone to prevent preterm birth in high-risk women. Natural
progesterone (17-alpha hydroxyprogesterone caproate) shown to reduce preterm birth rate
in women with prior spontaneous preterm delivery. Also beneficial for short cervix
detected on screening. Typically started in the second trimester.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Progestogen} A synthetic form of progesterone, the hormone that maintains the uterine lining in the
secretory phase of the menstrual cycle and supports early pregnancy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prognosis} Predicted outcome of cancer. Factors: stage, grade, molecular features, patient status.
Survivability statistics.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prognostic Test} A test that provides information about the likely course or outcome of a disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Progression} Worsening or advancement of a disease, such as increasing tumor size, new lesions, or spread to another site.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Progression-Free Survival} The length of time from a defined starting point, such as diagnosis or treatment, until
cancer progression or death from any cause, whichever occurs first.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Progressive Disease} Worsening of cancer during or after treatment, defined per RECIST as growth of target
lesions, appearance of new lesions, or unequivocal progression of non-target lesions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Progressive Supranuclear Palsy} A neurodegenerative tauopathy characterized by the accumulation of tau protein in the
basal ganglia, brainstem, and cerebral cortex, belonging to the family of Parkinson plus
syndromes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Projection} A defense mechanism in which unacceptable thoughts, feelings, or impulses are attributed
to others. For example, a patient who is angry at the therapist may accuse the therapist
of being angry at them. It is common in paranoid thinking, delusional disorders, and
personality disorders.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prolactin Physiology} Prolactin is the only pituitary hormone primarily under tonic inhibitory control by the
hypothalamus through dopamine acting on D2 receptors. Prolactin stimulates lactation
suppresses GnRH and is important for reproductive function. Physiological elevations
occur during pregnancy lactation breast stimulation sleep and stress.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prolactinoma} A benign prolactin-secreting pituitary adenoma, the most common functional pituitary
tumor, presenting with galactorrhea, amenorrhea, infertility, and hypogonadism in women,
and decreased libido, erectile dysfunction, and visual field defects in men. Prolactin
levels are markedly elevated, often above 200 ng per mL.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prolactinoma Treatment} Treatment may include a dopamine agonist, surgery, radiation, or observation,
depending on tumor size, symptoms, hormone levels, treatment response, and patient preferences. Dopamine agonists often
lower prolactin levels and reduce tumor size.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prolapse of orbital fat} Herniation of intraorbital fat through a dehiscence in the orbital septum, most commonly
presenting as a painless, soft, reducible mass in the upper eyelid or lower eyelid. It
is often age-related and may be confused with a lipoma or dermoid cyst.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prolapse of the Uterus (Uterine Prolapse)} Descent of the uterus into the vaginal canal due to weakening of pelvic floor supports,
most commonly the uterosacral and cardinal ligaments.



\textbf{Synonyms}: Uterine Prolapse

\medterm{Proliferative Diabetic Retinopathy} The advanced stage of diabetic retinopathy, characterized by the growth of new, fragile
blood vessels (neovascularization) on the retinal surface, optic disc, or iris in
response to severe retinal ischemia and the release of vascular endothelial growth
factor.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prolonged Deceleration} A fetal heart rate deceleration lasting \(\geq 2\) minutes but <10 minutes. If the
deceleration persists \(\geq 10\) minutes, it is a baseline change. Causes: maternal
hypotension (epidural-induced aortocaval compression), uterine hyperstimulation,
umbilical cord prolapse, placental abruption, and maternal seizure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prolonged Second Stage} Second stage of labor exceeding the time limits: nulliparas — 3 hours with regional
anesthesia (2 hours without); multiparas — 2 hours with regional (1 hour without).
Management: reassess position, contractions, and fetal status. For inadequate
contractions: oxytocin augmentation. For malposition (OP, OT): manual rotation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prominent iris collarette} A visible thickening or ridge of the iris at the junction of the pupillary and ciliary
zones, representing the site where the embryonic pupillary membrane was attached. It is
typically a normal anatomical variant but can be a clinical sign in conditions such as
Axenfeld-Rieger syndrome or iridocorneal endothelial syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Promoter} DNA sequence upstream of gene where RNA polymerase and transcription factors bind to
initiate transcription. Core promoter: TATA box, transcription start site. Regulatory
elements: enhancers, silencers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pronation} Rotation of the forearm so that the palm faces posteriorly in anatomical position, or
downward when the elbow is flexed. The radius crosses over the ulna.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prone Positioning} Turning supine patients to prone for > 12 hours daily in severe ARDS. Improves
oxygenation by recruiting dorsal lung segments. Reduces mortality when PaO2/FiO2 < 150.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Propafenone} A class IC antiarrhythmic with sodium channel blockade and weak beta-blocking activity.
Used for paroxysmal atrial fibrillation (in patients without structural heart disease,
including 'pill-in-the-pocket'), supraventricular tachycardias, and ventricular
arrhythmias.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Properdin Deficiency} X-linked recessive (CFP gene). Properdin stabilizes alternative pathway C3 convertase.
Recurrent Neisseria infections (meningococcemia, gonococcemia). Low AH50, normal CH50,
low properdin. Treatment: meningococcal vaccination (ACWY + B), prophylactic penicillin,
IVIG for severe infections.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prophylaxis} Preventive administration of a vaccine, antimicrobial, or immune globulin to prevent
infection in exposed or at-risk individuals. Examples: post-exposure prophylaxis for
rabies, HIV, and hepatitis B; perioperative antibiotic prophylaxis; and chemoprophylaxis
for malaria. See also Endocarditis Prophylaxis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Propofol Infusion Syndrome} Rare but fatal complication of prolonged high-dose propofol infusion. Features:
metabolic acidosis, rhabdomyolysis, cardiac failure, hyperlipidemia. Risk: critical
illness, children, high doses (>4-5 mg/kg/hr), prolonged infusion (>48 hours).
Prevention: limit dose and duration.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Proprioception} The sense of body position, movement, and joint position arising from mechanoreceptors
in muscles (muscle spindles), tendons (Golgi tendon organs), and joints, as well as skin
receptors. Signals ascend via the dorsal column-medial lemniscal pathway (conscious
proprioception) and the spinocerebellar tract (unconscious).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Propylthiouracil} A thioamide antithyroid drug that inhibits thyroid peroxidase (hormone synthesis) and
blocks peripheral T4-to-T3 conversion. Used for hyperthyroidism/Graves disease—preferred
in the first trimester of pregnancy (lower teratogenicity than methimazole) and in
thyroid storm.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prostacyclin Analogs} Medications that mimic prostacyclin (PGI2), causing vasodilation and inhibiting platelet
aggregation in the pulmonary vasculature. They include epoprostenol (continuous IV),
treprostinil (subcutaneous, IV, inhaled, oral), and iloprost (inhaled). They are used
for severe PAH.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prostaglandin Analogs} A class of topical medications used in the treatment of open-angle glaucoma and ocular
hypertension that lower intraocular pressure by increasing the uveoscleral outflow of
aqueous humor, reducing intraocular pressure by approximately twenty-five to thirty-five
percent.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prostaglandins} Lipid compounds involved in labor initiation and cervical ripening. Endogenous
prostaglandins (PGE2, PGF2alpha) stimulate uterine contractions and promote cervical
softening. Synthetic analogs used clinically: misoprostol (PGE1) and dinoprostone (PGE2
gel or insert) for cervical ripening and labor induction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prostate} Walnut-sized gland (approximately 20 g) surrounding the urethra just below the bladder,
producing approximately 30 percent of seminal fluid. It has peripheral, central, and
transition zones.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prostate Adenocarcinoma} The most common non-cutaneous malignancy in men and the second leading cause of cancer
death in men after lung cancer. The disease typically arises in the peripheral zone of
the prostate and is hormone-dependent, requiring androgens for growth.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prostate Biopsy} Sampling of prostate tissue, commonly by transperineal or transrectal needle technique,
for histologic diagnosis of prostate cancer. Targeted and systematic cores may be
combined, with attention to infection prevention, bleeding, urinary retention, and
sepsis risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prostate Cancer} Most common non-cutaneous cancer in men. Risk factors: age, family history, African
American race, high red meat intake. Screening: PSA testing, DRE. Diagnosis: transrectal
ultrasound-guided biopsy. Staging: TNM system, Gleason score.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prostate-Specific Antigen} A serine protease produced mainly by prostate epithelial cells and secreted into seminal
fluid. PSA can be measured in blood, where levels may rise with benign enlargement,
inflammation, infection, or prostate cancer. Interpretation depends on age, prostate size,
medicines, symptoms, and changes over time.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prostatectomy} Surgical procedure involving the partial or total removal of the prostate gland. Simple
prostatectomy: removal of the inner adenoma while leaving the peripheral capsule intact,
performed open, laparoscopically, or robotically for very large BPH (>80-100 grams).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prostatitis} Inflammation or infection of the prostate that may cause pelvic or urinary pain, difficulty urinating, fever, or recurrent
urinary infections. It can be acute, chronic bacterial, or chronic pelvic pain syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prosthesis} Artificial replacement for missing body part. Types: upper extremity, lower extremity.
Components: socket, suspension, joint, pylon, terminal device. Fitting: certified
prosthetist. Training: physical therapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prosthetic Limb} Artificial replacement for missing limb. Types: above/below knee, above/below elbow.
Components: socket, suspension, joint, pylon, terminal device. Materials: carbon fiber,
silicone, metal. Rehabilitation: gait training, strength, ADL.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prosthetic Training} Training for amputees: stump care, donning/doffing, gait training, balance, stair
negotiation, community ambulation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prosthetics} Artificial replacement of missing body part. Elderly use: lower limb (transtibial,
transfemoral), upper limb. Considerations: mobility level, cognition, vision,
comorbidities, support system. Rehabilitation: physical therapy, strength training,
balance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prosthodontics} Dental specialty concerned with replacement of missing teeth and restoration of oral
function. Types: fixed (crowns, bridges, implants), removable (partial dentures,
complete dentures), maxillofacial prosthetics (ocular, nasal, auricular). Goals:
aesthetics, function, phonetics, preservation of remaining structures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Protease inhibitor} A class of antiretroviral drugs that block the viral protease enzyme responsible for
cleaving polyprotein precursors into functional proteins during HIV replication,
preventing the formation of mature, infectious virions. Used in combination
antiretroviral therapy for HIV infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Protease Inhibitors} A class of antiviral drugs that block viral proteases, enzymes essential for processing
viral polyproteins into functional components needed for viral assembly and maturation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Protective Factor} Attribute decreasing probability of disease. Examples: exercise (cardiovascular
disease), vaccination (infectious disease).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Protein} Polymer of amino acids linked by peptide bonds. Structure: primary (sequence), secondary
(alpha helix, beta sheet), tertiary (3D folding), quaternary (multiple subunits).
Functions: enzymes, structural, transport, signaling, immune defense, movement. Folding
determined by amino acid sequence (Anfinsen's dogma). Chaperones assist folding.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Protein Binding} The reversible association of drug molecules with plasma proteins, primarily albumin for
acidic drugs and alpha-1 acid glycoprotein for basic drugs. Only unbound or free drug is
pharmacologically active and available for distribution to tissues, metabolism by the
liver, and excretion by the kidneys.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Protein C} Natural anticoagulant. Inactivates factors Va and VIIIa. Warfarin can cause skin
necrosis in deficiency.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Protein C Deficiency} An inherited (autosomal dominant) thrombophilia causing increased VTE risk. Protein C is
a vitamin K-dependent natural anticoagulant that inactivates factors Va and VIIIa and
stimulates fibrinolysis. Heterozygous: 6-10 fold increased VTE risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Protein Folding} The process by which newly synthesized polypeptide chains acquire their functional
three-dimensional structures. The amino acid sequence (primary structure) determines the
folding pathway.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Protein S} Vitamin K-dependent plasma glycoprotein that acts as a cofactor for activated protein C
in the inactivation of factors Va and VIIIa. Deficiency or dysfunction increases the
risk of venous thromboembolism.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Protein S Deficiency} An inherited (autosomal dominant) thrombophilia. Protein S is a vitamin K-dependent
natural anticoagulant that acts as a cofactor for activated protein C in inactivating
factors Va and VIIIa. Heterozygous: 2-10 fold increased VTE risk. Homozygous: rare,
purpura fulminans.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Protein Synthesis} Translation of mRNA code into polypeptide chain. Initiation: ribosome assembly at start
codon (AUG). Elongation: aminoacyl-tRNAs bring amino acids; peptide bonds form in
peptidyl transferase center; ribosome translocates. Termination: stop codon (UAA, UAG,
UGA) recognized by release factors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Protein-Energy Malnutrition} Severe deficiency of protein and calories. Types: marasmus (chronic, wasting),
kwashiorkor (acute, edema, fatty liver), marasmic-kwashiorkor. Risk: famine, poverty,
alcoholism, malignancy, malabsorption. Treatment: gradual refeeding, monitor refeeding
syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Proteinuria} Excretion of more than 150 mg of protein in urine daily. Causes include glomerular
disease, tubulointerstitial disease, overflow proteinuria from multiple myeloma, and
functional proteinuria from fever or exercise. Quantification by 24-hour urine or spot
protein-to-creatinine ratio.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prothrombin Time} PT: a coagulation test measuring the extrinsic and common pathways (factors VII, X, V,
II, fibrinogen), used to monitor warfarin therapy (reported as INR) and assess bleeding
risk. Prolonged PT: warfarin/Vitamin K deficiency, liver disease, DIC, factor
deficiencies (VII), and anticoagulant use.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Proto-Oncogene} A normal gene that regulates cell growth, division, differentiation, or survival and can
become an oncogene after activating mutation, amplification, translocation, or
overexpression.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Proton Pump Inhibitor} A class of medications that irreversibly inhibit the gastric H+/K+ ATPase (proton pump)
in parietal cells, the final common pathway of gastric acid secretion, producing
profound and sustained suppression of both basal and stimulated acid production.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Proton Therapy} Radiation using protons instead of photons. Bragg peak allows precise dose delivery.
Used for pediatric tumors, skull base tumors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Protozoa} Single-celled eukaryotes lacking a cell wall, classified by locomotion into amoebae,
flagellates, ciliates, and sporozoa.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Proximal} Positioned closer to the trunk of the body or point of origin of a limb.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Proximal Humerus Fractures} Proximal humerus fractures: Neer classification (4 parts: articular surface, greater
tuberosity, lesser tuberosity, shaft; displaced >1 cm or angulated >45 degrees = part).
1-part (nondisplaced): sling, early motion. 2-part: surgical neck (ORIF plate, IM nail)
or greater tuberosity (if displaced >5mm or rotator cuff involvement).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Proximal Radioulnar Joint} Pivot synovial joint between radial head circumference and ulnar radial notch, held by
the annular ligament. Allows pronation/supination with approximately 150 degrees range.
Parallel bones in supination; radius crosses ulna in pronation. Significant in radial
head fractures, Monteggia fracture-dislocations, and nursemaid's elbow.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Proximal Tubule} Segment of the nephron extending from Bowman capsule to the loop of Henle with a
convoluted proximal straight tubule and convoluted proximal tubule. Responsible for
reabsorbing approximately sixty-five percent of filtered sodium, water, bicarbonate,
glucose, amino acids, and phosphate through an extensive brush border.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Proximal Tubule Reabsorption} The proximal convoluted tubule (PCT) reabsorbs approximately 65 percent of filtered
sodium, water, glucose, amino acids, bicarbonate, and phosphate.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Proximate Cause} The legally sufficient cause that directly contributes to an injury or outcome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prune Belly Syndrome} A congenital syndrome comprising the triad: deficient abdominal wall musculature
(wrinkled, prune-like appearance), bilateral cryptorchidism (undescended testes), and
urinary tract anomalies (dilated ureters, dilated and trabeculated bladder, and
posterior urethral valves or urethral atresia). Almost exclusively affects boys.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pruritus} Itching: an unpleasant sensation provoking scratching, a common symptom of skin and
systemic disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pruritus Ani} Persistent itching around the anus with multiple potential causes including hemorrhoids,
fissures, infections such as pinworms and candidiasis, dermatologic conditions including
psoriasis and lichen sclerosus, and idiopathic causes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pruritus in CKD} Severe itching in dialysis patients from uremic toxin accumulation, elevated
calcium-phosphorus product, and secondary hyperparathyroidism. Difficult to treat.
Include phosphate binders, dialysis optimization, gabapentin, and UV phototherapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{PSA (Prostate-Specific Antigen)} A serine protease produced by prostate tissue and measured in blood. It may be used with
shared decision-making to assess prostate-cancer risk and to monitor known disease. PSA
can also rise with benign enlargement, inflammation, infection, or recent procedures, so
no single value establishes or excludes cancer.



\textbf{Synonyms}: Prostate-Specific Antigen

\medterm{Pseudoaneurysm} A contained arterial wall disruption in which blood communicates with the arterial lumen
but is held by surrounding tissue rather than all layers of the arterial wall. It may
follow catheterization, trauma, surgery, infection, or grafting and can rupture or
embolize.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pseudobulbar Affect} Involuntary episodes of laughing or crying disproportionate to mood, usually due to
neurologic disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pseudoclubbing} Broadening and flattening of the nail tips without the complete loss of the angle
between the nail and nail fold that characterizes true clubbing. It is seen in
conditions such as hyperparathyroidism, rheumatoid arthritis, and psoriasis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pseudoephedrine} A sympathomimetic (alpha-1 agonist, some beta effects) oral decongestant used for nasal
congestion in colds and sinusitis. Increases nasal mucosal vasoconstriction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pseudogout} Calcium pyrophosphate deposition disease (CPPD): deposition of calcium pyrophosphate
dihydrate crystals in joints, causing acute arthritis resembling gout (pseudogout) or
chronic osteoarthritis-like disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pseudogout Flare} Acute crystal-induced arthritis caused by calcium pyrophosphate dihydrate deposition,
often affecting the knee or wrist. Identification of positively birefringent rhomboid
crystals in synovial fluid supports the diagnosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pseudohypoparathyroidism} A group of inherited disorders in which target organs are resistant to parathyroid
hormone, causing hypocalcaemia and hyperphosphataemia despite elevated PTH. Some forms
cause Albright hereditary osteodystrophy, with short stature, round facies,
brachydactyly, or subcutaneous ossifications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pseudomelanosis duodeni} Benign condition characterized by dark pigment deposits in the duodenal mucosa, visible
as slate-gray or black patches on endoscopy, caused by accumulation of iron-containing
pigment in macrophages within the lamina propria.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pseudomembranous Colitis} Colonic inflammation caused by C. difficile infection showing yellow-white plaques on
colonoscopy. The plaques consist of fibrin, inflammatory cells, and necrotic epithelial
debris overlying mucosal ulceration. Presents with profuse watery diarrhea and abdominal
pain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pseudomonas aeruginosa infection} Infection caused by Pseudomonas aeruginosa, an opportunistic gram-negative bacterium
that can affect the lungs, wounds, urinary tract, blood, or other sites.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pseudomonas aeruginosa resistance} Pseudomonas aeruginosa has intrinsic and acquired resistance mechanisms making it
inherently resistant to many antibiotics. Intrinsic resistance includes low outer
membrane permeability, efflux pumps, and chromosomal beta-lactamase.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pseudomyxoma Peritonei} Accumulation of mucinous ascites and mucin-producing tumor nodules in the peritoneal
cavity, usually from a ruptured low-grade appendiceal or ovarian mucinous neoplasm;
treated with cytoreductive surgery and hyperthermic intraperitoneal chemotherapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pseudothrombocytosis due to red cell fragments} Falsely elevated platelet count on automated hematology analyzers caused by
fragmentation of red blood cells (schistocytes) being counted as platelets. Seen in
microangiopathic hemolytic anemias such as TTP, HUS, DIC, and mechanical heart valves.
Manual peripheral blood smear review confirms true platelet count.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pseudoxanthoma elasticum} An inherited connective tissue disorder caused by mutations in the ABCC6 gene, leading
to progressive calcification and fragmentation of elastic fibres in the skin, eyes, and
cardiovascular system. Cutaneous findings include yellowish, peau d'orange papules in
flexural areas (neck, axillae, groin) resembling plucked chicken skin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Psittacosis} A zoonotic infection caused by Chlamydia psittaci, transmitted from infected birds
(parrots, pigeons, poultry) via inhalation of dried bird droppings/secretions. Features:
abrupt fever, headache, myalgia, dry cough, atypical pneumonia; complications:
hepatitis, myocarditis, meningitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{PSMA} Prostate-specific membrane antigen. Target for PET imaging and therapy in prostate
cancer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Psoas Abscess} A collection of pus in or around the psoas muscle, caused by infection spreading through the blood or from nearby tissues.
It may cause back or hip pain, fever, difficulty walking, or few symptoms and usually
requires imaging and targeted treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Psoriasis} A chronic immune-mediated skin disorder that causes well-defined plaques with overlying scale. It may also affect the nails and
joints, and its severity and distribution vary among individuals.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Psoriatic Arthritis} An inflammatory arthritis associated with psoriasis of the skin or nails. It may affect
the spine, peripheral joints, digits, or tendon insertions, and diagnosis uses the clinical pattern together with
appropriate laboratory and imaging findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Psychoactive Substance} A substance that alters mood, perception, cognition, behavior, or consciousness.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Psychodermatology} Psychodermatology addresses the mind-skin connection. Stress exacerbates psoriasis,
eczema, acne, and urticaria. Psychiatric disorders (depression, anxiety, body dysmorphic
disorder) are associated with skin conditions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Psychodynamic Therapy} A form of depth psychology that explores how unconscious processes, early life
experiences, and internal conflicts influence current thoughts, feelings, and behaviors.
It involves exploration of transference and countertransference, defense mechanisms, and
the therapeutic relationship as a vehicle for change.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Psychogenic Non-Epileptic Seizures} Seizure-like episodes that are not caused by abnormal electrical activity in the brain.
They are a form of conversion disorder with attacks or seizures. Events may include
shaking, stiffening, unresponsiveness, and other movements.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Psychological Support} Counseling, therapy for patients with chronic illness, disability, pain. Address
depression, anxiety, adjustment difficulties, coping strategies.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Psychopharmacology} The study and clinical application of medications used to treat psychiatric disorders.
It involves understanding drug mechanisms, pharmacokinetics, drug interactions, and side
effect profiles.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Psychosis} A syndrome characterized by loss of contact with shared reality, commonly involving
delusions, hallucinations, disorganized thought or behavior, and negative symptoms.
Causes include primary psychotic disorders, mood disorders, substances, medications,
neurologic disease, and systemic illness.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Psychosocial Rehabilitation} Support for mental illness recovery: skills training, supported employment, peer
support.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Psychosocial Support} Cancer affects mental health. Depression, anxiety common. Support groups, counseling
beneficial.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Psychotherapy} A structured treatment in which a trained therapist helps a person understand and change
distressing thoughts, emotions, behaviors, or relationship patterns. Major approaches
include cognitive behavioral therapy, psychodynamic therapy, interpersonal therapy,
supportive therapy, exposure-based therapy, and family or group therapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pterygium} A triangular, fibrovascular proliferation of bulbar conjunctiva extending onto the
cornea, usually nasal, associated with chronic ultraviolet exposure, dust, and dry
climates. Most are asymptomatic or cause mild irritation, but growth toward the visual
axis can induce astigmatism and obstruct vision.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{PTH-Related Peptide} A peptide that activates the parathyroid-hormone receptor and can cause humoral
hypercalcemia of malignancy. It increases bone resorption and renal calcium retention
while reducing phosphate reabsorption.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pthirus pubis} The pubic louse, a parasitic insect that infests the pubic hair and occasionally other
coarse body hair, causing pediculosis pubis. Transmission is primarily through sexual
contact. It causes intense pruritus and may transmit Borrelia recurrentis causing
relapsing fever. Diagnosis is by visual identification of the louse or nits.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Ptosis} The drooping of the upper eyelid, which can be congenital or acquired, and can range
from mild drooping that is primarily a cosmetic concern to severe drooping that
obstructs the visual axis and causes amblyopia in children or visual impairment in
adults.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{PTSD} Post-traumatic stress disorder, a condition involving persistent trauma-related memories, avoidance, negative mood
or cognition, and heightened arousal after a severe traumatic event.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Puberty} Developmental period during which sexual maturation and reproductive capability are
attained.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pubis} Anterior-inferior hip bone with body, superior ramus, and inferior ramus. Two pubic
bones meet at the pubic symphysis (cartilaginous joint widening in childbirth). Pubic
tubercle is the lateral inguinal ligament attachment. Direct hernias emerge medial to
inferior epigastric vessels near it.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Public Exposure} Radiation exposure to public from nuclear medicine patients. Guidelines for discharge
after therapy. I-131 therapy: radiation safety instructions for 3-7 days.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Public Health} The science and practice of protecting and improving the health of communities through
education, policy-making, and research for disease and injury prevention.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Public Health Emergency} Event threatening public health. Examples: pandemic, bioterrorism, natural disaster.
Declaration enables special powers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Public Health Research Bias} Systematic error in a population-health study, including selection bias, information
bias, and confounding.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pudendal Block} A local anesthetic block of the pudendal nerve (S2-S4) used for perineal anesthesia
during the second stage of labor, outlet forceps delivery, or perineal repair.
Technique: transvaginal approach — a needle guide is placed on the ischial spine, and
the needle is advanced through the sacrospinous ligament.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Puerperal Fever} Fever higher than 38 degrees Celsius occurring during the first 10 days postpartum, not
including the first 24 hours. Most common cause is endometritis, particularly after
cesarean delivery. Other causes include urinary tract infection, wound infection,
mastitis, and thrombophlebitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Puerperal psychosis} A rare psychiatric emergency beginning soon after childbirth, characterized by rapidly
changing mood, confusion, delusions, hallucinations, or disorganized behavior.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Puerperium} The period following delivery during which the reproductive organs return to their
prepregnancy state, approximately 6 weeks in duration. Characterized by uterine
involution, cervical closure, lochial discharge changes, and hormonal shifts. Physical
recovery includes perineal healing, breast engorgement, and metabolic readjustment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pugilistic Stance} The characteristic body position adopted by fire victims—flexed arms, elbows bent, fists
clenched, knees flexed—resulting from heat-induced contraction (denaturation) of muscles
(mainly flexors) and tendons, not from conscious movement. It occurs with thermal injury
and can be present regardless of cause (even if death preceded fire).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Alveolar Hemorrhage} Bleeding into the alveolar spaces from disruption of the alveolar-capillary membrane.
Causes include vasculitis (GPA, MPA, Goodpasture), connective tissue disease,
medications, and coagulopathy. It presents with hemoptysis, falling hemoglobin, diffuse
bilateral infiltrates, and serially bloody returns on BAL.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Alveolar Microlithiasis} A rare genetic disease caused by SLC34A2 mutations, leading to accumulation of calcium
phosphate microliths within the alveoli. HRCT shows diffuse bilateral calcified nodules
with a ``sandstorm'' appearance. It is usually diagnosed in the third to fourth decade.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Alveolar Proteinosis} A rare disease characterized by accumulation of surfactant-like material within the
alveoli due to impaired clearance by alveolar macrophages. Most cases are autoimmune
(anti-GM-CSF antibodies). Presents with progressive dyspnea and cough. HRCT shows
bilateral ground-glass opacities with ``crazy paving'' pattern.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Angiography} Contrast imaging of the pulmonary arteries, usually by catheter. It can diagnose pulmonary vascular abnormalities but is now used less often for pulmonary embolism because CT pulmonary angiography is less invasive.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Arterial Hypertension} A precapillary pulmonary hypertension syndrome caused by increased pulmonary vascular
resistance from pulmonary arteriolar disease. It may be idiopathic, heritable,
drug-associated, or associated with conditions such as connective-tissue disease,
congenital heart disease, portal hypertension, or HIV.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Arteriovenous Fistula} An abnormal direct connection between a pulmonary artery and vein that bypasses the
capillary bed. It can cause hypoxemia, cyanosis, dyspnea, paradoxical emboli, or brain
abscess.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Arteriovenous Malformation} An abnormal direct communication between a pulmonary artery and vein, causing a
right-to-left shunt. It may be idiopathic or associated with hereditary hemorrhagic
telangiectasia (Osler-Weber-Rendu syndrome). It can cause hypoxemia, paradoxical emboli
(stroke, brain abscess), and hemoptysis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
