\medterm{Pulmonary Artery} The blood vessel that carries deoxygenated blood from the right ventricle of the heart
to the lungs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Artery Catheter} A catheter inserted through the right heart into the pulmonary artery to measure
hemodynamic parameters (PA pressure, PAWP, cardiac output). Used for diagnosis and
management of pulmonary hypertension, heart failure, and critical illness.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Atresia} A congenital heart defect where the pulmonary valve is completely absent (atretic),
obstructing right ventricular outflow. Two main types: pulmonary atresia with intact
ventricular septum (PA-IVS) and pulmonary atresia with ventricular septal defect
(PA-VSD, also called tetralogy of Fallot with pulmonary atresia).

\textbf{Synonyms}: tetralogy of Fallot with pulmonary atresia).

\medterm{Pulmonary Capillary Wedge Pressure} An indirect estimate of left-atrial pressure obtained by briefly occluding a small
pulmonary-artery branch with a balloon-tipped catheter. It reflects left-sided filling
pressure when measured correctly and interpreted in the appropriate clinical and
ventilatory context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Contusion} Bruising of the lung parenchyma from blunt chest trauma, causing hemorrhage and edema
without lung laceration. It impairs gas exchange and can progress to ARDS. It is often
associated with rib fractures and flail chest. Treatment is supportive with
lung-protective ventilation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Edema} Accumulation of fluid in the pulmonary interstitium and alveoli, most commonly due to
left-sided heart failure (cardiogenic). Causes increased hydrostatic pressure leads to
transudation. Presents with acute dyspnea, orthopnea, crackles, and S3 gallop.
Non-cardiogenic causes include ARDS, renal failure, and high altitude.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Embolism} Occlusion of the pulmonary arterial bed by a thrombus, tumor, air, or fat, most commonly
originating as a deep vein thrombosis (DVT) from the lower extremities. Presents with
acute onset of dyspnea, pleuritic chest pain, cough, hemoptysis, tachycardia, and
hypoxemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Embolism Treatment} Anticoagulation: DOACs preferred (rivaroxaban, apixaban) or LMWH bridging to warfarin.
Duration: 3 months for provoked (reversible risk factor), indefinite for unprovoked or
recurrent. Massive PE: systemic thrombolysis (alteplase 100 mg IV over 2 hours) or
surgical catheter-directed embolectomy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Fibrosis} Scarring replacing normal alveolar architecture. Causes: IPF, connective tissue disease,
asbestos, radiation, drugs (bleomycin, methotrexate). Features: progressive dyspnea, dry
cough, digital clubbing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Function Tests} Spirometry: FVC (forced vital capacity), FEV1 (forced expiratory volume in 1 second),
FEV1/FVC ratio. Obstructive pattern: FEV1/FVC <0.7 (asthma, COPD). Restrictive pattern:
FVC <80\% with normal or increased FEV1/FVC (pulmonary fibrosis, chest wall disorders).
Lung volumes: TLC (total lung capacity), RV (residual volume).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Hemosiderosis} Accumulation of iron-containing pigment in the lungs, usually after repeated bleeding into the air spaces. It may cause
cough, coughing blood, anemia, shortness of breath, or diffuse lung changes and can
result from immune-mediated or other disorders.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Hypertension} Elevated mean pulmonary artery pressure (>20 mmHg at rest) due to various etiologies.
WHO Group 1: pulmonary arterial hypertension (idiopathic, heritable, drug-induced).
Group 2: left heart disease. Group 3: lung disease. Group 4: chronic thromboembolic.
Group 5: multifactorial. Diagnosis requires right heart catheterization.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Hypertension Classification} WHO Classification: Group 1 (PAH: idiopathic, heritable, drug-induced, connective tissue
disease-associated). Group 2 (left heart disease: systolic/diastolic dysfunction,
valvular). Group 3 (lung disease/hypoxia: COPD, ILD, sleep apnea). Group 4 (chronic
thromboembolic). Group 5 (multifactorial: sarcoidosis, hematologic disorders).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Infarction} Lung necrosis from PE when bronchial supply is compromised. Causes pleuritic pain,
hemoptysis, and wedge-shaped opacity. Most PE do not cause infarction due to dual blood
supply.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Lobes} The anatomical divisions of the lungs separated by interlobar fissures. The right lung
has superior, middle, and inferior lobes; the left lung has superior and inferior lobes
and a lingula.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Rehabilitation} A supervised, multidisciplinary program combining exercise training, education,
behavioral support, and self-management for chronic respiratory disease. It improves
exercise capacity, dyspnea, functional status, and quality of life.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Sequestration} A congenital malformation of non-functioning lung tissue that lacks normal communication
with the tracheobronchial tree and receives its blood supply from a systemic artery
(usually the descending aorta). Intralobar sequestration is within the normal pleural
investment; extralobar is outside.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Stenosis} A right ventricular outflow tract obstruction at the valvular, subvalvular, or
supravalvular level. Valvular pulmonary stenosis (most common) is often congenital,
frequently associated with Noonan syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Surfactant} A phospholipid-protein mixture produced by type II alveolar pneumocytes that reduces
surface tension at the air-liquid interface within alveoli. The primary component is
dipalmitoylphosphatidylcholine (DPPC, lecithin), which forms a monolayer that reduces
surface tension to near zero during expiration.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary tuberculosis} Tuberculosis infection involving the lungs, usually caused by Mycobacterium tuberculosis and
spread through airborne particles.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Valve} Semilunar valve between right ventricle and pulmonary trunk with three cusps. Most
superiorly located cardiac valve with thinner cusps than the aortic valve. Pulmonic
stenosis is a congenital defect causing right ventricular hypertrophy. Less commonly
affected by endocarditis than mitral/aortic valves.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Vascular Resistance} The resistance to blood flow through the pulmonary circulation, calculated approximately
as the transpulmonary pressure gradient divided by cardiac output. Elevated resistance
contributes to pulmonary hypertension and right-ventricular afterload.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Vasodilator Therapy} Medications that reduce pulmonary vascular resistance and pulmonary artery pressure.
Used for WHO Group 1 PAH. Includes endothelin receptor antagonists (bosentan), PDE-5
inhibitors (sildenafil), prostacyclin analogs (epoprostenol), and soluble guanylate
cyclase stimulators (riociguat).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Vein} The four veins (two from each lung) returning oxygenated blood from the lungs to the
left atrium, a unique exception in the venous system (carrying oxygenated blood).
Superior and inferior pulmonary veins enter the left atrium posteriorly.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Wedge Pressure} The pressure measured by a balloon-tipped pulmonary artery catheter when the balloon is
inflated, occluding a branch of the pulmonary artery. It approximates left atrial
pressure. Normal is 6--12 mmHg. Elevated in left heart failure (PCWP > 18 mmHg). Low in
hypovolemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary-artery dissection} Rare but life-threatening tear in the pulmonary artery wall, most commonly associated
with pulmonary hypertension, Marfan syndrome, or prior pulmonary artery catheterization.
Presents with acute chest pain, dyspnea, and hemodynamic collapse. Diagnosis by CT
angiography or echocardiography; management requires emergent surgical repair.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulp} Soft connective tissue central to tooth containing blood vessels, nerves, connective
tissue. Responds to injury with inflammation, necrosis, calcification. Root canal
therapy removes infected pulp.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulp Stone} Calcified mass in pulp chamber or root canal. May be free, attached, or embedded.
Incidental finding.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulpectomy} Complete removal of pulp from primary tooth. Followed by resorbable filling material.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulposus} Relating to the nucleus pulposus, the gelatinous central portion of an intervertebral
disk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulpotomy} Removal of coronal pulp in primary tooth with reversible inflammation. Maintains
radicular pulp vitality.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulse} The rhythmic expansion of an artery produced by each heartbeat. Clinicians assess its rate, rhythm, strength, and symmetry.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulse Examination} Assessment of arterial pulses for rate, rhythm, strength, and symmetry to help evaluate circulation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulse Oximeter} A noninvasive device that estimates peripheral oxygen saturation using light absorption. Readings can be
affected by poor perfusion, motion, skin or nail characteristics, device limitations, and
the clinical setting.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulse Oximetry} A noninvasive method for estimating peripheral oxygen saturation by measuring red and infrared light
absorption through a pulsatile vascular bed. It is widely used for monitoring, but values
must be interpreted with symptoms, perfusion, device limitations, and the individual's
clinical targets.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulseless Electrical Activity} A cardiac arrest rhythm organized electrical activity without a detectable pulse. PEA
has many reversible causes (5 H's and 5 T's): hypovolemia, hypoxia, hydrogen ion
(acidosis), hypo/hyperkalemia, hypothermia; tension pneumothorax, tamponade (cardiac),
toxins, thrombosis (pulmonary), thrombosis (coronary).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulsus Paradoxus} A decrease in systolic blood pressure greater than 10 mmHg during inspiration, normally
less than 10 mmHg. Exaggerated in cardiac tamponade (due to ventricular interdependence
in the confined pericardial space), severe asthma, COPD exacerbation, and constrictive
pericarditis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Punch Biopsy} A small, full-thickness circular biopsy obtained with a rotating trephine, used to
diagnose skin and soft-tissue lesions. Suture closure may be needed.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Punctate Hemorrhages (Tardieu Spots)} Small, pinpoint sub-ecchymotic hemorrhages (petechiae) resulting from the rupture of
small capillaries under conditions of intense mechanical venous engorgement and
asphyxia.



\textbf{Synonyms}: Tardieu Spots

\medterm{Punctate palmoplantar keratoderma} A hereditary keratoderma characterised by multiple, small, firm, circumscribed keratotic
papules and plaques scattered across the palms and soles, often with an autosomal
dominant inheritance pattern. It may present in adolescence or adulthood and can be
associated with malignant transformation in rare cases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pupil} Central round iris aperture for light entry, size controlled by sphincter pupillae (CN
III parasympathetic, constricts to 2-3 mm) and dilator pupillae (sympathetic, dilates to
8 mm). The pupillary light reflex tests CN II (afferent) and CN III (efferent): light in
one eye causes bilateral constrination (direct and consensual).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pure Tone Audiometry} The standard behavioral test used to measure hearing sensitivity across the frequency
range important for speech perception, typically from 250 to 8000 Hertz, by determining
the lowest intensity level at which a pure tone can be detected approximately fifty
percent of the time (the hearing threshold).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Purging} Deliberate behaviors intended to compensate for eating, such as self-induced vomiting or misuse of laxatives,
diuretics, or excessive exercise.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Purging Disorder} Recurrent purging behavior to influence weight or shape in the absence of binge eating.
Self-induced vomiting, misuse of laxatives, diuretics, or other medications. It is
classified under Other Specified Feeding or Eating Disorder (OSFED). It can lead to
electrolyte imbalances, dental erosion, Russell's sign, and esophageal tears.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Purines} Nitrogenous bases containing a double-ring structure (a six-membered ring fused to a
five-membered imidazole ring), which serve as essential components of nucleotides in DNA
and RNA. The primary purines are adenine (A) and guanine (G).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Purkinje Fibers} The terminal branches of the cardiac conduction system, distributed throughout the
ventricular myocardium. They conduct impulses rapidly (up to 4 m/s) to ensure
coordinated ventricular contraction from apex to base. Purkinje fibers have an intrinsic
firing rate of 20-40 beats per minute.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Purpura} Purple discoloration of the skin or mucous membranes caused by extravasation of red
blood cells from blood vessels into the surrounding tissue. Purpura can be classified by
size: petechiae are less than 2 millimeters, purpura are 2 to 10 millimeters, and
ecchymoses are greater than 10 millimeters.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Purpura fulminans} A rapidly progressive, life-threatening condition characterised by extensive, confluent,
non-blanching purpuric skin necrosis with haemorrhagic bullae, resulting from acute
intravascular thrombosis and disseminated intravascular coagulation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Purulent Pericarditis} Bacterial infection of the pericardial space, most commonly caused by S. aureus, S.
pneumoniae, or gram-negative organisms. Often occurs as a complication of pneumonia,
endocarditis, chest surgery, or immunosuppression. Presents with fever, pleuritic chest
pain, and rapidly accumulating effusion with tamponade physiology.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pus} A thick, yellow or green liquid that is composed of dead cells and bacteria, most often
found at the site of a bacterial infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Push Enteroscopy} Endoscopic technique using a longer endoscope to visualize and intervene in the proximal
small bowel beyond the reach of standard upper endoscopy. Unlike capsule endoscopy, push
enteroscopy allows direct visualization with the ability to obtain biopsies and perform
hemostasis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pustule} A small, circumscribed elevation of skin containing pus (neutrophils) within the
epidermis. Causes: bacterial (folliculitis, impetigo, furuncle), acne vulgaris
(inflammatory comedones), pustular psoriasis (generalized pustular psoriasis—systemic
symptoms), hidradenitis suppurativa, and drug reactions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Putrefaction} Postmortem decomposition of body tissues brought about by bacterial and enzymatic
action, characterized by green discoloration of lower abdomen (cecum), marbling of
cutaneous veins, gas distension, foul odor, and liquefaction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pyelonephritis} An infection of the renal parenchyma and pelvicalyceal system, typically resulting from
ascending urinary tract infection. Acute pyelonephritis most commonly affects women and
is caused by gram-negative enteric organisms, particularly Escherichia coli, which
account for approximately 80 percent of cases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pyelotomy} An incision into the renal pelvis, usually made to remove an obstruction or kidney
stone.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pylephlebitis} Septic thrombophlebitis of the portal vein or its tributaries, most commonly arising
from intra-abdominal infections such as appendicitis, diverticulitis, cholangitis, or
hepatic abscess. Presents with high fever, rigors, right upper quadrant pain, jaundice,
and sepsis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pyloric Stenosis} Hypertrophy of the pyloric sphincter muscle causing gastric outlet obstruction most
common in young infants. Presents with non-bilious projectile vomiting typically
beginning at two to eight weeks of age. Dehydration and metabolic alkalosis with
hypochloremia and hypokalemia develop as the condition progresses.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pylorus} The muscular outlet of the stomach that connects to the duodenum. Its sphincter regulates the passage of partially
digested food into the small intestine; narrowing or blockage can cause vomiting, abdominal distension,
and dehydration, especially in infants with pyloric stenosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pyoderma Gangrenosum} A neutrophilic inflammatory skin disorder that causes painful ulcers with undermined,
violaceous borders. It may be associated with inflammatory bowel disease, arthritis, blood disorders, or other systemic
conditions, and trauma can worsen the lesions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pyogenic Granuloma} A benign vascular proliferation presenting as a rapidly growing, friable, red papule or
nodule that bleeds easily. It commonly occurs on fingers, face, and lips, and may be
triggered by trauma or pregnancy (epulis gravidarum). Diagnosis is clinical.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pyonephrosis} Pus in renal collecting system. Urological emergency. Requires drainage (stent or
nephrostomy) and antibiotics.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pyostomatitis vegetans} A rare, chronic, inflammatory oral mucosal condition characterized by multiple, soft,
yellowish, vege tative pustules on an erythematous base, predominantly affecting the
buccal mucosa and gingiva. It is strongly associated with inflammatory bowel disease,
particularly ulcerative colitis, and may precede gastrointestinal symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pyrexia} A regulated rise in body temperature caused by changes in the hypothalamic temperature set point. See Fever.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pyridoxine (Vitamin B6)} A vitamin converted to pyridoxal phosphate, a coenzyme for transamination,
decarboxylation, and other amino-acid reactions. Deficiency can cause neuropathy,
dermatitis, seizures, and sideroblastic anemia; excessive supplementation can also cause
neuropathy.



\textbf{Synonyms}: Vitamin B6

\medterm{Pyrimidines} Nitrogenous bases containing a single six-membered heterocyclic ring, serving as
structural building blocks for nucleic acids. The primary pyrimidines are cytosine (C),
thymine (T, present in DNA), and uracil (U, present in RNA).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pyruvate} The three-carbon alpha-keto acid produced at the end of glycolysis from
phosphoenolpyruvate by pyruvate kinase.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pyruvate Dehydrogenase} Converts pyruvate to acetyl-CoA. Regulated by phosphorylation (inactivated by PDH
kinase). Requires thiamine, lipoate, CoA, FAD, NAD+.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pyruvate Kinase Deficiency} An inherited red-cell glycolytic defect causing chronic hemolytic anemia of variable
severity. Manifestations include jaundice, splenomegaly, gallstones, fatigue, and
complications of iron overload.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pannus} An abnormal layer of vascularized tissue that grows over a joint or the cornea.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Paraplegia} Paralysis of both legs and usually part of the lower trunk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Parasomnias} Unwanted movements, behaviors, emotions, or experiences that occur during sleep or at the transition between sleep and wakefulness.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Parathyroid Hormone} A hormone that regulates calcium and phosphate levels in the blood and supports bone remodeling.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Paroxysmal Hemicrania} A rare headache disorder causing brief, repeated attacks of severe one-sided head or facial pain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Passive Immunity} Protection against infection provided by antibodies received from another source rather than produced by the person's own immune system.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Patch Test} A skin test used to identify delayed allergic contact reactions to substances applied under adhesive patches.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pattern-Recognition Receptors} Immune receptors that detect common molecular features of pathogens or damaged cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{PDE5 Inhibitors} Medicines that increase penile blood flow by inhibiting phosphodiesterase type 5; they are used mainly to treat erectile dysfunction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Percutaneous Diskectomy} Removal of part of a herniated spinal disk through a small skin incision.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Perforation} An abnormal hole through the wall of an organ or tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Perfusion Defect} An area receiving abnormally low blood flow, identified by an imaging or functional test.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Peyronie's Disease} A condition in which scar tissue in the penis causes curvature or pain during erection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Phagocytes} Immune cells that engulf and digest microorganisms, dead cells, and other particles.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Phonophobia} Abnormal sensitivity to or fear of sound, often associated with migraine.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Photorefractive Keratectomy} Laser eye surgery that reshapes the cornea to correct refractive errors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Physical Dependence} A state in which the body adapts to a substance or medication and develops withdrawal symptoms when it is reduced or stopped.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pick's Disease} A form of frontotemporal dementia characterized by progressive changes in behavior, language, and personality.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Platelet} A small blood component that helps form clots and stop bleeding.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Posterior Myocardial Infarction} A heart attack involving the back wall of the heart.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postherpetic Neuralgia} Persistent nerve pain in an area affected by shingles after the rash has healed.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primary Hypertension} High blood pressure without an identifiable single underlying cause.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Procedural Memory} Long-term memory for learned skills and procedures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prodrome} An early symptom or group of symptoms that precedes a disease episode.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prolapsed Disk} A spinal disk that bulges or ruptures and may compress a nearby nerve; also called a herniated disk.

\textbf{Synonyms}: a herniated disk.

\medterm{Proliferative Retinopathy} Advanced retinal disease in which abnormal new blood vessels grow and may bleed or impair vision.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Proptosis} Forward displacement or bulging of an eye.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prostatic Urethral Stent} A small device placed in the prostatic urethra to help maintain urine flow when the prostate obstructs it.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Proctocolitis} Inflammation of the rectum and the adjacent portion of the colon.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Emphysema} A chronic lung disease in which damage to the air sacs reduces the lungs' ability to exchange gases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Park-Bench Position} A modified lateral position in which the upper body is partly rotated toward prone; it is used for selected posterior brain and neck procedures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prone Position} A position in which the patient lies face down, commonly providing access to the back, spine, buttocks, or posterior legs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pancolitis} Inflammation affecting the entire colon, often as an extensive form of ulcerative colitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pancreatic Cysts} Fluid-filled sacs in or around the pancreas, which may be benign or precancerous.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Panic Attacks and Panic Disorder} Panic attacks are sudden episodes of intense fear; panic disorder involves recurrent attacks and persistent concern about having more.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Parotid Tumors} Abnormal growths arising in the parotid salivary glands, located in front of and below the ears.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Parvovirus Infection} An infection caused by parvovirus B19 that commonly causes mild fever and rash but may be serious in some people.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Peanut Allergy} An immune reaction to peanut proteins that can cause symptoms ranging from hives to life-threatening anaphylaxis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pediatric Brain Tumors} Tumors that develop in the brain or nearby tissues of children.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pediatric Obstructive Sleep Apnea} Repeated blockage of a child's upper airway during sleep, causing pauses in breathing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Penicillin Allergy} An immune reaction to penicillin antibiotics that may cause rash, swelling, breathing difficulty, or anaphylaxis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Peripheral Nerve Tumors} Tumors that arise from or around nerves outside the brain and spinal cord.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postconcussion Syndrome} Persistent physical, cognitive, or emotional symptoms after a concussion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Precordium} The region of the chest overlying the heart.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pyuria} An abnormal number of white blood cells or pus in the urine, often associated with urinary infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Philadelphia Chromosome} A chromosome rearrangement, usually t(9;22), that creates the BCR::ABL1 fusion gene and is characteristic of most cases of chronic myeloid leukemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Phonocardiography} Graphic recording of heart sounds and murmurs using a microphone and recording system.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Phorometer} An instrument used to measure ocular alignment and the amount of prism correction needed for binocular vision disorders.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Photosensitization} Increased sensitivity to ultraviolet or visible light caused by a medication, chemical, or disease, producing an exaggerated skin reaction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Physical Examination} A systematic assessment of the body using inspection, palpation, percussion, auscultation, and other examination methods.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Physical Therapy} Rehabilitation using exercise, movement training, manual techniques, and physical modalities to improve function, mobility, and reduce disability.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Phytomedicine} The evidence-based use of medicinal products derived from plants to prevent or treat disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pineal Gland} A small endocrine gland in the brain that secretes melatonin and helps regulate circadian rhythms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pitting Edema} Swelling in which pressure applied to the skin leaves a temporary indentation, usually because of excess interstitial fluid.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Placental Abnormalities} Disorders of placental location, attachment, structure, or separation that may affect pregnancy or cause bleeding.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Placenta Previa} Implantation of the placenta over or near the internal cervical opening, which can cause painless bleeding during the second half of pregnancy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Plasmin} A proteolytic enzyme that breaks down fibrin and helps dissolve blood clots.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Plethysmography} Measurement of changes in the volume of a body part or organ, used to assess blood flow, lung volumes, or other physiologic functions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pleural Disorders} Conditions affecting the pleura, including pleural effusion, pneumothorax, pleurisy, infection, and tumors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pleural Space} The potential space between the visceral and parietal pleura surrounding each lung.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pneumonitis} Inflammation of lung tissue, often caused by infection, inhaled substances, radiation, medications, or immune reactions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pneumoplasty} Surgical repair or reconstruction of lung tissue or an airway.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pneumotachometer} An instrument that measures the rate of airflow during breathing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postural Drainage} Positioning the body so gravity helps move respiratory secretions toward the larger airways for clearance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prepuce} The fold of skin covering the glans penis; it is also called the foreskin.

\textbf{Synonyms}: the foreskin.

\medterm{Protein Electrophoresis} A laboratory test that separates proteins in serum or urine according to their electrical properties, helping detect abnormal protein patterns.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pseudophakia} The presence of an artificial intraocular lens after removal of the natural lens, usually during cataract surgery.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Circuit} The circulation in which blood travels from the right ventricle to the lungs for gas exchange and returns to the left atrium.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pulmonary Ventilation} The movement of air into and out of the lungs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pyramidal Tracts} Descending motor pathways from the cerebral cortex that control voluntary movement, especially precise movement of the limbs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Palate} The roof of the mouth, consisting of a bony hard palate in front and a muscular soft palate behind.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Palatine Tonsils} Paired masses of lymphoid tissue on the sides of the oropharynx that contribute to immune defense.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pancreatic Islets} Clusters of endocrine cells in the pancreas that release hormones such as insulin, glucagon, somatostatin, and pancreatic polypeptide.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Panhypopituitarism} Deficiency of most or all anterior pituitary hormones due to disease of the pituitary gland or hypothalamus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Paralysis} Loss of voluntary muscle movement in part or all of the body, caused by disruption of motor pathways or muscles.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Parenchyma} The functional tissue of an organ, distinguished from its supporting connective tissue, or stroma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pelvimetry} Measurement of the dimensions of the maternal pelvis, usually to assess selected obstetric concerns.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Peripartum} The period immediately before, during, and shortly after childbirth.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Peyer Patches} Aggregated lymphoid follicles in the mucosa and submucosa of the ileum that monitor intestinal contents and support immune defense.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Nullipara} A person who has never given birth at or beyond the gestational age considered viable, regardless of the outcome of any pregnancies.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primipara} A person who has given birth once at or beyond the gestational age considered viable, regardless of whether the infant was born alive.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Primigravida} A person who is pregnant for the first time.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Nulligravida} A person who has never been pregnant.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pes Anserinus} The combined tendon insertion of the sartorius, gracilis, and semitendinosus muscles on the anteromedial proximal tibia; inflammation there is pes-anserine bursitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pilonidal Cyst} A cyst or sinus in the cleft between the buttocks, usually containing hair and skin debris. It may become infected, painful, swollen, and drain pus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Polyneuropathy} A generalized disorder affecting multiple peripheral nerves, often causing symmetric numbness, tingling, burning pain, weakness, or impaired reflexes in the limbs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Puncture Wound} A wound produced when a pointed object penetrates the skin and underlying tissue, creating a small surface opening with potentially deep tissue damage and infection risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Parathyroid Carcinoma} A rare malignant tumor of the parathyroid gland that commonly causes excessive parathyroid-hormone secretion, hypercalcemia, kidney stones, bone disease, or related symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Paraparesis}Weakness affecting both legs, usually caused by disease or injury of the spinal cord, nerve roots, or peripheral nerves.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Paratyphoid Fever}An enteric fever caused by Salmonella Paratyphi, producing prolonged fever and gastrointestinal or systemic illness similar to typhoid fever.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pasteurellosis}Infection caused by Pasteurella bacteria, commonly introduced through animal bites or scratches and causing cellulitis, abscesses, or occasionally invasive disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Parietal Bone}One of two skull bones forming much of the roof and sides of the cranium.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pathophysiology}The study of the functional changes produced by disease or injury and the mechanisms through which they cause signs and symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pavor Nocturnus (Night Terrors)}Episodes of sudden intense fear, screaming, and autonomic arousal during sleep, usually arising from deep non-REM sleep and often followed by little or no memory.



\textbf{Synonyms}: Night Terrors

\medterm{Pectoriloquy}Abnormally clear transmission of spoken sounds through the chest wall, often suggesting lung consolidation or another change in lung tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Penumbra}Brain tissue surrounding an area of infarction that is functionally impaired but potentially salvageable if blood flow is restored promptly.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Post-Natal Depression}Depression beginning during pregnancy or after childbirth, with persistent low mood or loss of interest and possible effects on sleep, bonding, and daily functioning.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Post-Partum Haemorrhage}Excessive bleeding after childbirth, most commonly caused by failure of the uterus to contract, retained placental tissue, genital-tract trauma, or clotting problems.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pre-Eclampsia}A pregnancy-related disorder involving new hypertension after 20 weeks of gestation with proteinuria or other evidence of maternal organ or placental dysfunction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Premature Birth}Birth before 37 completed weeks of pregnancy; the earlier the birth, the greater the risks of respiratory, neurological, and developmental complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Presbyacusis (Presbycusis)}Age-related, usually gradual sensorineural hearing loss, particularly affecting the ability to hear high-frequency sounds and understand speech in noise.



\textbf{Synonyms}: Presbycusis

\medterm{Pseudocyesis}A false belief or bodily state resembling pregnancy, with symptoms such as abdominal enlargement, breast changes, or even perceived fetal movement despite no fetus being present.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prothrombin Time (PT)}A blood test measuring the time required for clot formation through the extrinsic and common coagulation pathways; it is commonly reported as the INR.



\textbf{Synonyms}: PT

\medterm{Pregnancy of Unknown Location} A positive pregnancy test without a confirmed intrauterine or ectopic pregnancy on ultrasound. Serial human chorionic gonadotropin measurements and repeat imaging help determine whether the pregnancy is early, failing, or ectopic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subchorionic Hemorrhage} Bleeding between the chorion and the uterine wall caused by partial separation of the gestational sac membranes. It may produce vaginal bleeding in early or mid-pregnancy and is assessed according to bleeding, gestational age, and ultrasound findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postpartum Preeclampsia} New-onset hypertension with pre-eclampsia features after childbirth, sometimes occurring several days or weeks postpartum. Severe headache, visual symptoms, chest pain, shortness of breath, or seizures require urgent assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Pelvic Girdle Pain in Pregnancy} Pain arising from the pelvic joints and surrounding tissues during pregnancy, often affecting the pubic symphysis, sacroiliac joints, hips, or lower back. It may impair walking, turning in bed, or climbing stairs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Round Ligament Pain} Sharp or aching pain in the lower abdomen or groin caused by stretching of the round ligaments as the uterus enlarges during pregnancy. Persistent, severe, or bleeding-associated pain requires medical evaluation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postpartum Care} Clinical care during the weeks after childbirth that assesses bleeding, uterine involution, blood pressure, infection, wound healing, breastfeeding, mental health, contraception, and recovery.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postpartum Urinary Retention} Inability to empty the bladder adequately after childbirth, sometimes associated with epidural anesthesia, perineal trauma, swelling, or prolonged labor. Prompt assessment prevents bladder overdistension and injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Postpartum Contraception} Contraceptive counseling and care after childbirth, taking account of breastfeeding, venous-thromboembolism risk, medical conditions, fertility intentions, and the timing of method initiation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Perinatal Asphyxia} Impaired oxygenation or ventilation before, during, or shortly after birth that may cause neonatal encephalopathy, organ injury, or death. Immediate newborn assessment and resuscitation are essential.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Prenatal Genetic Counseling} A consultation that reviews the risk of inherited or chromosomal conditions, available screening and diagnostic tests, possible results, and reproductive options before or during pregnancy.

\medterm{pagetoid reticulosis} pagetoid reticulosis is a medical or biomedical term used in clinical practice, research, anatomy, or health care; its interpretation depends on context.

\medterm{pain perception} pain perception is a medical condition or clinical entity; assessment and management depend on its cause, severity, and the individual patient.

\medterm{pain threshold} pain threshold is a medical condition or clinical entity; assessment and management depend on its cause, severity, and the individual patient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{palatal muscle} palatal muscle is a medical or biomedical term used in clinical practice, research, anatomy, or health care; its interpretation depends on context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{palate neoplasm} palate neoplasm is a medical condition or clinical entity; assessment and management depend on its cause, severity, and the individual patient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{palatine tonsil} palatine tonsil is a medical or biomedical term used in clinical practice, research, anatomy, or health care; its interpretation depends on context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{palatorrhaphy} palatorrhaphy is a medical or biomedical term used in clinical practice, research, anatomy, or health care; its interpretation depends on context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{palifermin} palifermin is a medical or biomedical term used in clinical practice, research, anatomy, or health care; its interpretation depends on context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{palivizumab} palivizumab is a medicine or pharmacologic substance; its indication, dose, and safety depend on the specific clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{palladium} palladium is a medical or biomedical term used in clinical practice, research, anatomy, or health care; its interpretation depends on context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{palm oil} palm oil is a medical or biomedical term used in clinical practice, research, anatomy, or health care; its interpretation depends on context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{palmar reflex} palmar reflex is a medical or biomedical term used in clinical practice, research, anatomy, or health care; its interpretation depends on context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{palmitic acid} palmitic acid is a medical or biomedical term used in clinical practice, research, anatomy, or health care; its interpretation depends on context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{palmoplantar hyperkeratosis} palmoplantar hyperkeratosis is a medical or biomedical term used in clinical practice, research, anatomy, or health care; its interpretation depends on context.
